<h1 class="ltx_title ltx_title_document">TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs</h1>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract"><h6 class="ltx_title ltx_title_abstract">Abstract.</h6>
    
<p id="abstract1.1" class="ltx_p">We introduce <math id="abstract1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, a <math id="abstract1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate hierarchical agglomerative clustering (HAC) algorithm which scales to trillion-edge graphs.
Our algorithm is based on a new approach to computing <math id="abstract1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC, which is a novel combination of the nearest-neighbor chain algorithm and the notion of <math id="abstract1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC.
Our approach allows us to partition the graph among multiple machines and make significant progress in computing the clustering within each partition before any communication with other partitions is needed.</p>
    
<p id="abstract1.2" class="ltx_p">We evaluate <math id="abstract1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on a number of real-world and synthetic graphs of up to 8 trillion edges.
We show that <math id="abstract1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> requires over 100x fewer rounds compared to previously known approaches for computing HAC.
It is up to 8.3x faster than SCC, the state-of-the-art distributed algorithm for hierarchical clustering, while achieving 1.16x higher quality.
In fact, <math id="abstract1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> essentially retains the quality of the celebrated HAC algorithm while significantly improving the running time.</p>
  
</div>
<section id="S1" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1. </span>Introduction</h2>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Hierarchical agglomerative clustering (HAC) is a widely-used clustering algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib46" title="" class="ltx_ref">murtagh2012algorithms</a>; <a href="#bib.bib47" title="" class="ltx_ref">murtagh2017algorithms</a>; <a href="#bib.bib44" title="" class="ltx_ref">mullner2011modern</a>; <a href="#bib.bib45" title="" class="ltx_ref">mullner2013fastcluster</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">gronau2007optimal</a>; <a href="#bib.bib55" title="" class="ltx_ref">stefan1996multiple</a>)</cite> known for its high quality in a variety of applications <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib61" title="" class="ltx_ref">zhao2002evaluation</a>; <a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">hua2017mgupgma</a>; <a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">kobren2017hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib11" title="" class="ltx_ref">blundell2013bayesian</a>; <a href="#bib.bib19" title="" class="ltx_ref">culotta2007author</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">The algorithm takes as input a collection of <math id="S1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> points, as well as a function giving similarities between pairs of points.
At a high level, the algorithm works as follows. Initially, each point is put in a separate cluster of size <math id="S1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math>.
Then, the algorithm runs a sequence of steps.
In each step, the algorithm finds a pair of most similar clusters and merges them together, that is, the two clusters are replaced by their union.
Here, the similarity between the clusters is computed based on the similarities between the points in the clusters.
The exact formula used is configurable and referred to as <em id="S1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">linkage function</em>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Common linkage functions include <em id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">single-linkage</em> – the similarity between two clusters <math id="S1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S1.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math> is the maximum similarity of two points belonging to <math id="S1.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S1.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math>, and <em id="S1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">average-linkage</em> – the similarity between <math id="S1.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S1.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math> is the total similarity between points in <math id="S1.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S1.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math> divided by <math id="S1.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|C|\cdot|D|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>C</mi><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>D</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|C|\cdot|D|</annotation></semantics></math>.
Due to the particularly good empirical quality, HAC using average linkage similarity is of particular interest <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib61" title="" class="ltx_ref">zhao2002evaluation</a>; <a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">hua2017mgupgma</a>; <a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">kobren2017hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib43" title="" class="ltx_ref">moseley-wang</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" title="" class="ltx_ref">hac-reward</a>; <a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p4.1" class="ltx_p">The output of the algorithm is a <em id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">dendrogram</em> – a rooted binary tree (or a collection thereof) describing the merges performed by the algorithm.
The leaves of the tree correspond to the initial clusters of size 1, and each internal vertex represents a cluster obtained by merging its two children.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p5.1" class="ltx_p">Up until recently, a major shortcoming of HAC was very limited scalability, as the best known algorithms required time that is quadratic in the number of input points.
In addition to that, it was commonly assumed that the input to the algorithm is a complete <math id="S1.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n\times n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n\times n</annotation></semantics></math> matrix giving similarities between all input points, which posed another scalability barrier.<span id="footnote1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup>
            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          Even if only a linear number of pairs of points had nonzero similarity, the previously known algorithms would still require quadratic time.</span></span></span></p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p6.1" class="ltx_p">Recent results overcome the quadratic-time bottleneck by
allowing <math id="S1.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximation <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
A HAC algorithm is <math id="S1.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate if in each step it merges together two clusters whose similarity is within a <math id="S1.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math> factor from the maximum similarity between two clusters (at that point).</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p7.1" class="ltx_p">By allowing approximation and assuming that the input to the algorithm is a sparse similarity graph (e.g. the k-nearest neighbors graph of the input points), it was shown that HAC can be implemented in near-linear time <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>)</cite> and efficiently parallelized <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
At the same time, both relaxations (approximation and using a sparse graph) were shown not to negatively affect the empirical quality of the obtained solutions <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
This line of work led to single machine parallel HAC implementations scaling to graphs containing several billion vertices and about 100 billion edges <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p8" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p8.1" class="ltx_p">Nevertheless, some large-scale applications require clustering even larger datasets.
To address these needs, several distributed hierarchical clustering algorithms have been proposed <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib7" title="" class="ltx_ref">NIPS2017_2e1b24a6</a>; <a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>; <a href="#bib.bib31" title="" class="ltx_ref">7184911</a>; <a href="#bib.bib32" title="" class="ltx_ref">jin2013disc</a>; <a href="#bib.bib56" title="" class="ltx_ref">sumengen2021scaling</a>)</cite>.
These algorithms can handle up to tens of billions of datapoints and several trillion edges.
However, in order to provide high quality results and/or provable guarantees on the quality of the output, the algorithms need to run typically a significant number of parallel rounds on large-scale datasets <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>; <a href="#bib.bib56" title="" class="ltx_ref">sumengen2021scaling</a>)</cite>, and for algorithms that can run in few rounds, no provable quality guarantees are known <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>)</cite>.
An intriguing open question, then, is whether it is possible to achieve provable quality guarantees for hierarchical clustering, while running in very few rounds.</p>
</div>
<section id="S1.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">1.1. </span>Our Contribution</h3>

<div id="S1.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this paper we introduce <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> – a <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC algorithm, which runs in very few rounds and is thus amenable to a distributed implementation.
We obtain <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> by developing a new approach to computing <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC, which may be of independent interest.
We evaluate <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> empirically and show that our implementation scales to datasets of tens of billions of points and trillions of edges.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Our key algorithmic contribution is a new approach to computing <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC, which we now outline.
The exact (<math id="S1.SS1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate) HAC algorithm defines a very specific order, in which cluster merges must be performed – only a highest similarity pair of clusters can be merged in each step.
However, the very same output dendrogram (up to different tiebreaking) can be computed using a (parallel) RAC algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib56" title="" class="ltx_ref">sumengen2021scaling</a>)</cite> (which is based on the idea behind the nearest-neighbor chain algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">benzecri1982construction</a>; <a href="#bib.bib60" title="" class="ltx_ref">parchain</a>)</cite>).
In each step, the RAC algorithm finds all reciprocally most similar clusters.
That is, it finds each pair of clusters <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math>, such that <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math> is the most similar cluster to <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math>, and <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> is the most similar cluster to <math id="S1.SS1.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math> (for simplicity, let us assume that there are no ties).
It is easy to see that the reciprocally most similar pairs of clusters form a matching.
Then, the algorithm merges both clusters of each pair in parallel.
Clearly, this approach allows potentially making multiple merges in one step, some of which may merge pairs of clusters whose similarity is much smaller than the maximum similarity between two clusters.
Crucially, this yields the same result as the basic algorithm, even though both algorithms may perform merges in a very different order.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p3.1" class="ltx_p">We generalize the nearest-neighbor chain/RAC algorithm to a <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate algorithm, by introducing a notion of a <em id="S1.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">good</em> merge.
Namely, we call the merge of two clusters <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good if it satisfies a certain condition (see <a href="#S3.Thmtheorem1" title="Definition 0 (Good merge). ‣ 3. Approximate Nearest-Neighbor Chain Algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).
Crucially, whether a merge of two clusters is good can be checked locally, essentially by looking at the clusters and their incident edges.
We show that by performing the <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good merges in any order (for example, in parallel) one obtains a <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate dendrogram, i.e., one that can be obtained by performing a sequence of <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate merges.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p4.1" class="ltx_p">To put our algorithmic result in context, let us compare the amount of parallelism available to different HAC algorithms by looking at what edges can be merged in the beginning of the algorithm.
Fig. <a href="#S1.F1" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1.1. Our Contribution ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> compares the amount of parallelism in the RAC algorithm, <math id="S1.SS1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> and ParHAC.
ParHAC is a parallel <math id="S1.SS1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate parallel HAC algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>)</cite>, which allows merging any edge whose weight is at most a <math id="S1.SS1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math> factor away from the globally maximum weight.
If we consider the sets of edges that can be merged by RAC and ParHAC, it is easy to see that none of them is a superset of the other one.
At the same time, the set of edges that TeraHAC can merge is a superset of both sets (this is the case in general, not just in the provided example).
As shown in Fig. <a href="#S1.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 1.2. Further Related Work ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> the increased parallelism leads to over 100x reduction in the number of rounds.
Importantly, the reduction in the number of rounds also yields between 3.5–10.5x improvements in running time as shown in Fig. <a href="#S1.F3" title="Figure 3 ‣ 1.2. Further Related Work ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/2308.03578/assets/terahac-merges-figure-noparallel.svg" id="S1.F1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="175" height="70"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1. </span><span id="S1.F1.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Comparison of the merges available at the start of the algorithm for different parallel HAC algorithms.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S1.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p5.1" class="ltx_p">We evaluate <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on a number of graph datasets of up to <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="8" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>8</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">8</annotation></semantics></math> trillion edges.
We first study its quality on several smaller datasets with ground truth.
We show that when using <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, the difference in quality compared to exact HAC is only <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1\%" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1\%</annotation></semantics></math>–<math id="S1.SS1.p5.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="3\%" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">3\%</annotation></semantics></math> (according to standard clustering similarity measures, ARI and NMI), which is in line with previous results on <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
Moreover, <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is more scalable and accurate than <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>)</cite> – the state of the art distributed algorithm for hierarchical graph clustering; it is on average 8.3x faster than <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>’s highest-quality setting (using 100 rounds)
on a set of large real-world web graphs. For the same high-quality setting, we find that <math id="S1.SS1.p5.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> achieves 1.16x higher quality than
<math id="S1.SS1.p5.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p6.1" class="ltx_p">Second, we study the scalability of <math id="S1.SS1.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on a number of large graphs.
We show that for <math id="S1.SS1.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> the number of rounds <math id="S1.SS1.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> runs is very low (at most 17 rounds over all our datasets), and that the residual graph shrinks at a geometric rate.
We also find that allowing approximation yields over an order of magnitude more <math id="S1.SS1.p6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good edges (edges that <math id="S1.SS1.p6.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> can potentially merge), compared with edges mergeable by RAC (see Fig. <a href="#S6.F15" title="Figure 15 ‣ 6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">15</span></a>).</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p7.1" class="ltx_p">Finally, we study the performance and quality of <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on an 8 trillion-edge graph representing similarities between 31 billion web queries using a set of human-generated labels.
We observe that <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> performs consistently better along a precision-recall curve, in some cases improving recall by up to <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="20\%" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>20</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">20\%</annotation></semantics></math> (relative), while being <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="30\%" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>30</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">30\%</annotation></semantics></math>–<math id="S1.SS1.p7.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="50\%" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>50</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">50\%</annotation></semantics></math> faster.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S1.SS2" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">1.2. </span>Further Related Work</h3>

<div id="S1.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">The HAC algorithm is particularly useful for semantic clustering applications, where the desired number of clusters is not known upfront, for example for de-duplication or, more generally, detecting groups of closely related entities <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib61" title="" class="ltx_ref">zhao2002evaluation</a>; <a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">hua2017mgupgma</a>; <a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>; <a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">kobren2017hierarchical</a>)</cite>.
In these cases in large-scale applications the number of clusters of interest is very high (close to the number of input entities), and so the average cluster size is low <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">kobren2017hierarchical</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p2.1" class="ltx_p">For other prominent applications of clustering, multiple successful algorithms have been developed.
For example DBSCAN <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib52" title="" class="ltx_ref">schubert2017dbscan</a>)</cite> and correlation clustering <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">bansal2004correlation</a>)</cite> are particularly well suited for finding anomalously dense clusters.
Modularity clustering <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib48" title="" class="ltx_ref">newman2006modularity</a>)</cite> is widely used for community detection.
Graph partitioning methods <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib9" title="" class="ltx_ref">partitioning2013charles</a>; <a href="#bib.bib13" title="" class="ltx_ref">bulucc2016recent</a>)</cite> split the data into a typically small number of roughly-equally sized clusters, while ensuring that related entities are not split across different clusters (hence, it is in a sense a dual problem to the one addressed by HAC).
Finally, k-means/k-median/k-center methods as well as spectral clustering <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib49" title="" class="ltx_ref">ng2001spectral</a>; <a href="#bib.bib23" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhillon2004kernel</a>)</cite> are particularly well-suited when the desired number of clusters is known upfront.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">The notion of approximate HAC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib41" title="" class="ltx_ref">48657</a>)</cite> was used to give more efficient HAC algorithms in the case when the input is a metric space <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib41" title="" class="ltx_ref">48657</a>; <a href="#bib.bib42" title="" class="ltx_ref">pmlr-v130-moseley21a</a>; <a href="#bib.bib59" title="" class="ltx_ref">DBLP:conf/icml/YaroslavtsevV18</a>; <a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">NEURIPS2019_d98c1545</a>)</cite> as well as a graph <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p4.1" class="ltx_p">For average linkage HAC, to the best of our knowledge, the fastest single-machine implementations scale to tens of millions of points in metric space <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib60" title="" class="ltx_ref">parchain</a>)</cite> or billion-vertex graphs <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p5.1" class="ltx_p">A number of graph clustering problems have been studied in the distributed setting, for example in MapReduce <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib21" title="" class="ltx_ref">62</a>)</cite>, Pregel/Giraph <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib38" title="" class="ltx_ref">malewicz2010pregel</a>; <a href="#bib.bib51" title="" class="ltx_ref">sakr2016large</a>)</cite> and other frameworks captured by the MPC model of computation <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">karloff2010model</a>)</cite>.
Examples include efficient algorithms for correlation clustering <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">10.1145/2623330.2623743</a>; <a href="#bib.bib18" title="" class="ltx_ref">pmlr-v139-cohen-addad21b</a>)</cite>, connected components <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">lkacki2018connected</a>)</cite> and balanced partitioning <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib4" title="" class="ltx_ref">10.14778/3324301.3324307</a>; <a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">10.1145/2835776.2835829</a>)</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p6.1" class="ltx_p">For HAC, efficient distributed algorithms are known for the single-linkage variant <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib31" title="" class="ltx_ref">7184911</a>; <a href="#bib.bib32" title="" class="ltx_ref">jin2013disc</a>; <a href="#bib.bib59" title="" class="ltx_ref">DBLP:conf/icml/YaroslavtsevV18</a>)</cite>.
We note that single-linkage hierarchical agglomerative clustering generally delivers worse quality compared to average linkage <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib61" title="" class="ltx_ref">zhao2002evaluation</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" title="" class="ltx_ref">hac-reward</a>)</cite>.

Several HAC algorithms have been recently developed in the shared-memory setting, including <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, a nearly linear time <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate sequential HAC algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>)</cite> and <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, a nearly linear work <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC algorithm with low depth <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
<math id="S1.SS2.p6.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> provably runs in poly-logarithmic rounds <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>, whereas <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> uses a novel relaxation of the reciprocal clustering approach of RAC, and neither <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> nor RAC are known to admit non-trivial round-complexity bounds.
However, empirically <math id="S1.SS2.p6.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> uses the fewest rounds of any modern HAC algorithm we study (see Figure <a href="#S5.F8" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a>).</p>
</div>
<figure id="S1.F2" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/2308.03578/assets/terahac_vs_parhac_vs_rac.svg" id="S1.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="143" height="96"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2. </span><span id="S1.F2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Number of rounds used by <math id="S1.F2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> compared with
<math id="S1.F2.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{OptimizedRAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖮𝗉𝗍𝗂𝗆𝗂𝗓𝖾𝖽𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{OptimizedRAC}</annotation></semantics></math> (<math id="S1.F2.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using <math id="S1.F2.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>),
<math id="S1.F2.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>
and
<math id="S1.F2.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{RAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{RAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on four large real-world graph datasets. All algorithms use a weight
threshold of <math id="S1.F2.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math> (see Section <a href="#S6" title="6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>).
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<figure id="S1.F3" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/2308.03578/assets/terahac_vs_optrac.svg" id="S1.F3.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="143" height="96"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3. </span><span id="S1.F3.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">
Distributed running times of <math id="S1.F3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> compared with
<math id="S1.F3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{OptimizedRAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖮𝗉𝗍𝗂𝗆𝗂𝗓𝖾𝖽𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{OptimizedRAC}</annotation></semantics></math> (<math id="S1.F3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using <math id="S1.F3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>) on the same graphs and threshold
as Figure <a href="#S1.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 1.2. Further Related Work ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S1.SS2.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p7.1" class="ltx_p">The most scalable hierarchical clustering algorithms have been obtained by slightly diverging from the HAC algorithm definition.
Affinity clustering <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib7" title="" class="ltx_ref">NIPS2017_2e1b24a6</a>)</cite>, and its successor SCC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>)</cite> are two highly scalable hierarchical clustering algorithms designed for the MapReduce model.
Both take graphs as input and were shown to scale to graphs with trillions of edges.
While both algorithms are similar to HAC by design, in practice they can give very different results from what the HAC algorithm gives.
We compare with SCC in our empirical analysis.
We conclude that while SCC can be tuned to obtain quality which is comparable with HAC, this comes at a cost of significantly higher running time of SCC.
We note that SCC was shown to deliver higher quality than affinity clustering.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS2.p8" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS2.p8.1" class="ltx_p">Another highly scalable HAC implementation is the RAC algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib56" title="" class="ltx_ref">sumengen2021scaling</a>)</cite> obtained by parallelizing the nearest neighbor chain algorithm.
The algorithm was shown to scale to datasets of billion points.
Since RAC is based on the exact HAC algorithm, the number of rounds it uses is an order of magnitude larger than what can be achieved by <math id="S1.SS2.p8.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>, which negatively affects performance.
As we show, <math id="S1.SS2.p8.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using <math id="S1.SS2.p8.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math> can be viewed as an optimized implementation of RAC, which we refer to as <math id="S1.SS2.p8.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{OptimizedRAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖮𝗉𝗍𝗂𝗆𝗂𝗓𝖾𝖽𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{OptimizedRAC}</annotation></semantics></math>.
At the same time <math id="S1.SS2.p8.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using <math id="S1.SS2.p8.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math> uses up to two orders of magnitude fewer rounds (see Figure <a href="#S1.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 1.2. Further Related Work ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>).</p>
</div>
</section>
</section>
<section id="S2" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2. </span>Preliminaries</h2>

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Let <math id="S2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G=(V,E,w)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G=(V,E,w)</annotation></semantics></math> be a weighted and undirected graph, where <math id="S2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|V|=n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>V</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|V|=n</annotation></semantics></math>.
We assume that all edge weights of <math id="S2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> are positive.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Let us now describe how the HAC algorithm works given <math id="S2.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> as an input.
Initially there are <math id="S2.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> clusters, each containing a distinct vertex of <math id="S2.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math>.
The algorithm proceeds in at most <math id="S2.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n-1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n-1</annotation></semantics></math> steps.
In each step, the algorithm finds two clusters of nonzero similarity and merges them together.
Let <math id="S2.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}</annotation></semantics></math>, be a sequence of clusterings, where <math id="S2.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is the clustering obtained after running <math id="S2.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="i-1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">i-1</annotation></semantics></math> steps of the algorithm.
In particular, <math id="S2.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1}</annotation></semantics></math> is the clustering containing <math id="S2.p2.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> clusters of size <math id="S2.p2.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">We define a sequence of graphs <math id="S2.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>G</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}</annotation></semantics></math>, where <math id="S2.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is obtained from <math id="S2.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> by contracting the clusters <math id="S2.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>.
Specifically, the vertex set of <math id="S2.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is <math id="S2.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>, which means that each vertex of <math id="S2.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is a <em id="S2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">set</em> of vertices of <math id="S2.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math>.
We define the function <math id="S2.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w:2^{V}\times 2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>V</mi></msup><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>V</mi></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w:2^{V}\times 2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics></math> giving edge weights in <math id="S2.p3.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> as <math id="S2.p3.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(u,{v})=\sum_{xy\in((u\times v)\cap E)}w(xy)/(|u|\cdot|v|)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∩</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></msub><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>u</mi><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(u,{v})=\sum_{xy\in((u\times v)\cap E)}w(xy)/(|u|\cdot|v|)</annotation></semantics></math>. Note that the weight function <math id="S2.p3.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>w</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w</annotation></semantics></math> is the same for all graphs <math id="S2.p3.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>. There is an edge between two vertices <math id="S2.p3.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p3.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="S2.p3.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> if and only if <math id="S2.p3.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(u,v)&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(u,v)&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_smallcaps ltx_title_theorem">Definition 0 (<cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">benzecri1982construction</a>)</cite>).</h6>
<div id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">A cluster similarity function <math id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="f:2^{V}\times 2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>V</mi></msup><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>V</mi></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">f:2^{V}\times 2^{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics></math> is called <em id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_upright">reducible</em> if and only if for any clusters <math id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x,y,z" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>z</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x,y,z</annotation></semantics></math>, we have <math id="S2.Thmtheorem1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="f(x,y\cup z)\leq\max(f(x,y),f(x,z))" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo>∪</mo><mi>z</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">f(x,y\cup z)\leq\max(f(x,y),f(x,z))</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p4.1" class="ltx_p">In particular, the function <math id="S2.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>w</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w</annotation></semantics></math> defined above is reducible <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">benzecri1982construction</a>)</cite>.
While in the following we work with this specific similarity function, we note that our main theoretical results hold for any similarity function satisfying Definition <a href="#S2.Thmtheorem1" title="Definition 0 ((
            , 
          )). ‣ 2. Preliminaries ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, which includes
e.g. single linkage, complete linkage and median linkage.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p5.1" class="ltx_p">Note that <math id="S2.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i+1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i+1}</annotation></semantics></math> is obtained from <math id="S2.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> by contracting a single edge and updating the weights of the edges incident to the vertex created by the contraction.
We call each such operation a <em id="S2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">merge</em> of the endpoints of the contracted edge.
Let <math id="S2.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{max}(G_{i})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>x</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{max}(G_{i})</annotation></semantics></math> be the largest edge weight in <math id="S2.p5.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>.
We say that a merge of an edge <math id="S2.p5.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="uv" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">uv</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="S2.p5.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is <math id="S2.p5.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate, if <math id="S2.p5.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)w(uv)\geq w_{max}(G_{i})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>w</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>x</mi></mrow></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)w(uv)\geq w_{max}(G_{i})</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p6.1" class="ltx_p">A <em id="S2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">dendrogram</em> is a tree describing all merges performed by running a HAC algorithm.
The tree has between <math id="S2.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="2n-1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2n-1</annotation></semantics></math> nodes<span id="footnote2" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">2</sup>
            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">2</span>
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          We use <em id="footnote2.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">nodes</em> when talking about trees and <em id="footnote2.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">vertices</em> in the context of graphs.</span></span></span>
corresponding to clusters that are created in the course of the algorithm.
Specifically, there are <math id="S2.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> leaf nodes corresponding to the initial singleton clusters.
In addition, for each merge that creates a cluster <math id="S2.p6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x\cup y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∪</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x\cup y</annotation></semantics></math> by merging <math id="S2.p6.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p6.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>, there is a node <math id="S2.p6.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x\cup y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∪</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x\cup y</annotation></semantics></math>, whose children are <math id="S2.p6.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p6.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>.
Hence, each internal node of the dendrogram has exactly two children.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p7.1" class="ltx_p">In the description of the algorithm, instead of using a dendrogram, it is more convenient to use a <em id="S2.p7.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">merge tree</em>.
Observe that if we remove all leaf nodes from the dendrogram, there is a natural 1-to-1 correspondence between the remaining nodes and the merges made by the algorithm.
We call the tree obtained this way, in which each node is a <em id="S2.p7.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">merge</em>, a merge tree.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p8" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p8.1" class="ltx_p">Observe that there may be multiple different <em id="S2.p8.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">sequences</em> of merges made by the algorithm that produce the very same merge tree (and dendrogram).
Specifically, given a fixed merge tree, all we know is that the first merge made by the algorithm must have been some merge corresponding to one of its leaves.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p9" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p9.1" class="ltx_p">Given a merge tree, and a sequence <math id="S2.p9.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>m</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}</annotation></semantics></math> of <math id="S2.p9.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math> distinct merges, we say that the sequence is <em id="S2.p9.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">consistent</em> with the tree, if and only if for each <math id="S2.p9.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1\leq i\leq k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>≤</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1\leq i\leq k</annotation></semantics></math>, either <math id="S2.p9.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is a leaf in the merge tree, or <math id="S2.p9.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is an internal node of the merge tree and the children of <math id="S2.p9.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> are <math id="S2.p9.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{a}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>a</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{a}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p9.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{b}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mi>b</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{b}</annotation></semantics></math>, where <math id="S2.p9.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\max(a,b)&lt;i" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>a</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>b</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mi>i</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\max(a,b)&lt;i</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p10" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p10.1" class="ltx_p">We say that a dendrogram is <em id="S2.p10.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic"><math id="S2.p10.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate</em> if and only if there exists a sequence of <math id="S2.p10.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate merges, such that (a) the sequence is consistent with the merge tree of the dendrogram, and (b) after performing all merges in the sequence, no pair of clusters has nonzero similarity.
Finally, a <math id="S2.p10.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC algorithm is one which produces a <math id="S2.p10.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate dendrogram.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S3" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3. </span>Approximate Nearest-Neighbor Chain Algorithm</h2>

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this section we extend the 40-year old nearest-neighbor chain algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">benzecri1982construction</a>)</cite> (NN-chain) with the notion of approximation.
Let <math id="S3.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> be an edge-weighted graph.
For each vertex <math id="S3.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> of <math id="S3.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math>, we denote by <math id="S3.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> the maximum weight of any edge incident to <math id="S3.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>.
It follows from the NN-chain / RAC algorithm that the following algorithm is equivalent to the (exact) HAC algorithm.
As long as the graph has nonzero edges, pick any edge <math id="S3.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="uv" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">uv</annotation></semantics></math>, such that <math id="S3.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)=w_{\max}(u)=w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)=w_{\max}(u)=w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> and merge together its endpoints.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Somewhat surprisingly, even though the HAC algorithm specifies a concrete order of merging edges, the NN-chain algorithm computes the very same dendrogram (up to ties among edge weights) as the standard HAC algorithm.
At the same time, the NN-chain algorithm has an important feature: the decision on whether to merge two vertices can be made entirely locally, which is a very useful property in a parallel setting.
In this section, we give an <math id="S3.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC algorithm also based on a simple local criterion for deciding whether two vertices can be merged, which we define below.</p>
</div>
<div id="S3.Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_smallcaps ltx_title_theorem">Definition 0 (Good merge).</h6>
<div id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Let <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon\geq 0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon\geq 0</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> be a graph obtained from <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> by performing some sequence of merges.
For each vertex <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> of <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>, we define <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathrm{M}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathrm{M}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> to be the smallest linkage similarity among all merges that were performed to create cluster <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>.
Specifically, for each singleton cluster <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> we have <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathrm{M}(v)=\infty" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathrm{M}(v)=\infty</annotation></semantics></math>, and whenever two clusters <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> are merged and create cluster <math id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u\cup v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>∪</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u\cup
