<section id="S6.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.1. </span>Quality</h3>

<div id="S6.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">We start by understanding the quality of <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> as a function of both <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>
and the weight threshold <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>.
All results in this section can be reproduced using our shared-memory implementation.
We compare the quality
to known ground-truth clusterings for the
<em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">iris</em>, <em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">wine</em>, <em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">digits</em>,
and <em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">faces</em> classification datasets
from the UCI dataset repository (found in the sklearn.datasets package).
Unfortunately, we are not aware of other large-scale publicly
available datasets providing ground truth clustering labels.
Our goal in this sub-section is to understand the largest
values of <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math> that can be used without damaging accuracy. Note that in our
experiments, weights in the graph are always within <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math>.
To measure quality, we use the
<em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">Adjusted Rand-Index (ARI)</em> and
<em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.6" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">Normalized Mutual Information (NMI)</em> scores,
which are standard measures of the quality of a clustering with respect
to a ground-truth clustering.
We also use the <em id="S6.SS1.p1.1.7" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">Dendrogram Purity</em> measure <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib29" title="" class="ltx_ref">heller05bayesian</a>)</cite>,
which takes on values
between <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="[0,1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">[0,1]</annotation></semantics></math> and takes on a value of <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math> if and only if the tree contains
the ground truth clustering as a tree consistent partition (i.e., each
class appears as exactly the leaves of some subtree of the tree).
Given a tree <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math> tree with leaves <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>V</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">V</annotation></semantics></math>, and a ground truth partition of
<math id="S6.SS1.p1.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>V</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">V</annotation></semantics></math> into <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{l}\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>l</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{l}\}</annotation></semantics></math> classes, define the purity
of a subset <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="S\subseteq V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊆</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">S\subseteq V</annotation></semantics></math> with respect to a class <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> to be <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{P}(S,C_{i})=|S\cap C_{i}|/|S|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>∩</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>S</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{P}(S,C_{i})=|S\cap C_{i}|/|S|</annotation></semantics></math>.
Then, the purity of <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math> is</p>
<table id="S6.Ex6" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="S6.Ex6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathcal{P}(T)=\frac{1}{|\emph{Pairs}|}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{x,y\in C_{i},x\neq y}\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{lca}_{T}(x,y),C_{i})" display="block" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>T</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>P</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>r</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mfrac><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><munderover><mo movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>l</mi></munderover><mrow><munder><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>≠</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow></mrow></munder><mrow><mi class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">𝒫</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>𝗅𝖼𝖺</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{P}(T)=\frac{1}{|\emph{Pairs}|}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{x,y\in C_{i},x\neq y}\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{lca}_{T}(x,y),C_{i})</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="S6.SS1.p1.2" class="ltx_p">where <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\emph{Pairs}=\{(x,y)\ |\ \exists i\text{ s.t. }\{x,y\}\subseteq C_{i}\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0.222em">​</mo><mi>P</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>a</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>r</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo rspace="0.222em" stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.667em">|</mo><mrow><mrow><mo rspace="0.167em">∃</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mtext> s.t. </mtext><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>⊆</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\emph{Pairs}=\{(x,y)\ |\ \exists i\text{ s.t. }\{x,y\}\subseteq C_{i}\}</annotation></semantics></math> and
<math id="S6.SS1.p1.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{lca}_{T}(x,y)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>𝗅𝖼𝖺</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{lca}_{T}(x,y)</annotation></semantics></math> is the set of leaves of the least common ancestor of <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m19" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m20" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m21" class="ltx_Math" alttext="T" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>T</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">T</annotation></semantics></math>.
We also study the unsupervised <em id="S6.SS1.p1.2.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">Dasgupta Cost</em> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib20" title="" class="ltx_ref">Dasgupta2016</a>)</cite> measure of our dendrograms,
which is measured with respect to an underlying similarity graph <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m22" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G(V,E,w)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G(V,E,w)</annotation></semantics></math>
and is defined as:
<math id="S6.SS1.p1.m23" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\sum_{(u,v)\in E}|\mathsf{lca}_{T}(u,v)|\cdot w(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo lspace="0em" stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>𝗅𝖼𝖺</mi><mi>T</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\sum_{(u,v)\in E}|\mathsf{lca}_{T}(u,v)|\cdot w(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
The Dasgupta cost is computed over the complete similarity graph obtained
by computing all point-to-point distances.
Lastly,
given a dendrogram and its greedy merge sequence we compute for each merge
the ratio between the highest similarity edge in the graph and the similarity of the
merge, and take the maximum of these ratios to be the <em id="S6.SS1.p1.2.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_bold ltx_font_italic">Empirical Approximation Ratio</em> (see Lemma <a href="#S3.Thmtheorem5" title="Lemma 0. ‣ 3. Approximate Nearest-Neighbor Chain Algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>).
For a <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m24" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate algorithm, the empirical approximation ratio is
at most <math id="S6.SS1.p1.m25" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1+\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1+\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Before jumping in, we make a few observations.
If we use a weight threshold of <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0</annotation></semantics></math>, the algorithm will compute a complete
<math id="S6.SS1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-HAC dendrogram (similar to <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> or <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>), but as we shall
see in Section <a href="#S6.SS2" title="6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.2</span></a>, computing the full <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-dendrogram
results in the size of the graph shrinking more slowly, and thus requires
more rounds.
On the other hand, using a weight threshold of <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math> will result in the algorithm
potentially running much faster due to the size of the graph shrinking rapidly (as
we show in Section <a href="#S6.SS2" title="6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.2</span></a>). However, from a quality perspective, a worry is
that in exchange for speed, we will sacrifice quality. In this sub-section, we will
understand how to set <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>, and will demonstrate that this worry
is unfounded. We will show the following key points:</p>
<ol id="S6.I1" class="ltx_enumerate">
<li id="S6.I1.i1" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">(1)</span> 
<div id="S6.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.I1.i1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Without thresholding, values of <math id="S6.I1.i1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon\in[0,0.5]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.5</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon\in[0,0.5]</annotation></semantics></math>
yield results that are typically within a few percent of the accuracy of
the exact algorithm.
Based on our results, we choose a value of <math id="S6.I1.i1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> for our experiments.</p>
</div></li>
<li id="S6.I1.i2" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">(2)</span> 
<div id="S6.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.I1.i2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Fixing <math id="S6.I1.i2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>, values of <math id="S6.I1.i2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t\leq 0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>≤</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t\leq 0.01</annotation></semantics></math> reliably
achieve very high quality on our datasets with ground-truth.</p>
</div></li>
</ol>
</div>

</span></figcaption>
</figure></div><div class="ltx_flex_break"></div><div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">

</span></figcaption>
</figure></div></div>
</figure>
<div id="S6.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S6.SS1.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Tuning <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>.</span>
We ran <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with varying values of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>
without thresholding to understand what value of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> is sufficient
for achieving good quality relative to the exact baseline of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>.
The experiment runs <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on similarity graphs constructed
from a <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math>-NN graph of the
underlying pointset, with <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k=25" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>25</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k=25</annotation></semantics></math>, as described earlier in Section <a href="#S6" title="6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>, and consistent with prior work <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
We use all 4 UCI datasets mentioned earlier;
due to space constraints we show a representative result for the digits dataset
in Fig. <a href="#S5.F8" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> for all quality measures.
Perhaps surprisingly, Fig. <a href="#S5.F8" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> shows that
the quality measures do not degrade significantly as <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> is increased.
We noticed similar trends for other values of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math>; the reason is likely due to
the fact that in our experiments, <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> makes very similar merges to <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>
even when using larger values of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>.
We select the value <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> to be consistent with the choice of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>
in prior papers on approximate HAC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite>.
Across all datasets, we found that for <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon\leq 0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>≤</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon\leq 0.1</annotation></semantics></math>
on average the outputs are within 1.3% of the best ARI score,
within 0.25% of the best NMI score, within 2.6% of the best
Purity score, and within 1.6% of the best Dasgupta score.
Fig. <a href="#S5.F8" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> empirically
confirms our theoretical
result that the approximation factor is always within a factor of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>.
For the remainder of the paper, we use a value of <math id="S6.SS1.p3.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> unless mentioned otherwise.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S6.SS1.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Tuning the Threshold (<math id="S6.SS1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>) with <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>.</span>
Computing the complete dendrogram can be unnecessary if the flat clustering we seek uses a large threshold (i.e., does not merge edges with similarity below <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>).
As Fig. <a href="#S5.F8" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> illustrates, larger thresholds require fewer rounds to compute, and can thus significantly improve the running time.
To understand how to select an appropriate threshold, <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>, we run <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with varying values of <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>, while fixing <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>.
Fig. <a href="#S5.F8" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> shows a representative result on the iris dataset, showing essentially
no difference across <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t\in[0,0.1]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t\in[0,0.1]</annotation></semantics></math>.
We tuned the threshold and find that for <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math>, across all
datasets, the accuracy results are very close to that of <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0</annotation></semantics></math>;
on average within 0.4% of the ARI
score of <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0</annotation></semantics></math>, within 2.6% of the Purity
score of <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0</annotation></semantics></math>, and within 1.6% of the Dasgupta cost of <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0</annotation></semantics></math>.
We find that <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math> achieves the same NMI as <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0</annotation></semantics></math>.
We set <math id="S6.SS1.p4.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math> in the remainder of the paper.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S6.F12" class="ltx_figure"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/2308.03578/assets/shared_memory_scalability.svg" id="S6.F12.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="191" height="107"></object>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 12. </span><span id="S6.F12.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Relative performance of <math id="S6.F12.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> vs. <math id="S6.F12.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.F12.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, all using <math id="S6.F12.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>. <math id="S6.F12.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is the original algorithm, and <math id="S6.F12.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>-Prune is the original algorithm using vertex pruning.
</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S6.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S6.SS1.p5.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Quality Comparison.</span>
Table <a href="#S6.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> shows the results of our quality study when
comparing <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with different settings of <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math> with the SCC
algorithm.
The non-zero threshold we use <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(t=0.01)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(t=0.01)</annotation></semantics></math> is tuned as described above.
The SCC algorithm can vary the number of rounds, <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>r</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r</annotation></semantics></math>, that is uses, which results
in <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>r</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r</annotation></semantics></math> flat clusterings.
We compute the quality of SCC by evaluating every flat clustering it produces and
using the best score across any clustering (i.e., for <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r=25" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>25</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r=25</annotation></semantics></math> we evaluate all 25
flat clusterings).
We also compare with the exact <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{2})</annotation></semantics></math> time average-linkage metric HAC implementation from
sklearn <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib54" title="" class="ltx_ref">scipyhac</a>)</cite> as well as the DBSCAN implementation from sklearn <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib53" title="" class="ltx_ref">scipydbscan</a>)</cite>.
Both algorithms compute the full distance matrix and thus run in <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{2})</annotation></semantics></math> time.
For DBSCAN, we set <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{minpts}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝗆𝗂𝗇𝗉𝗍𝗌</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{minpts}</annotation></semantics></math> by searching over the range
<math id="S6.SS1.p5.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon\in[0.01,10000]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mn>0.01</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>10000</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon\in[0.01,10000]</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{minpts}\in[2,128]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>𝗆𝗂𝗇𝗉𝗍𝗌</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>2,128</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{minpts}\in[2,128]</annotation></semantics></math>.
We consider four settings for <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>; <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0,t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0,t=0</annotation></semantics></math>, which yields an
exact HAC dendrogram that is equivalent to the <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{RAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{RAC}</annotation></semantics></math> algorithm; <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0,t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0,t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math>;
<math id="S6.SS1.p5.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1,t=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1,t=0</annotation></semantics></math>; and lastly, using <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m19" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1,t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1,t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math>.
All settings of <math id="S6.SS1.p5.m20" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> achieve high-quality
results across all of the quality measures that we evaluate, across
all datasets.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS1.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS1.p6.1" class="ltx_p">Our results in Table <a href="#S6.T2" title="Table 2 ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> show that <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> achieves
quality within a few percentage points of the best result on all datasets:
we find that for <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon\leq 0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>≤</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon\leq 0.1</annotation></semantics></math>
on average the outputs are within 1.3% of the best ARI score,
within 0.25% of the best NMI score, within 2.6% of the best
Purity score, and within 1.6% of the best Dasgupta score.
The results for the <em id="S6.SS1.p6.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">exact</em> parameter settings of <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>
show that thresholding using <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math> has little effect.
We also observe that SCC is similarly affected, and this algorithm, which
is the prior state-of-the-art in scalable HAC, also requires careful selection of <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math>.
SCC generally improves in quality as we increase the number of
compression rounds, with the exception of the digits dataset, where <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r=5" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r=5</annotation></semantics></math> achieves
good quality.
Comparing SCC to <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>,
<math id="S6.SS1.p6.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math><math id="S6.SS1.p6.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="{}_{\epsilon=0.1}^{t=0.01}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mmultiscripts><mphantom></mphantom><mprescripts></mprescripts><mrow></mrow><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><mrow></mrow></mmultiscripts><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{}_{\epsilon=0.1}^{t=0.01}</annotation></semantics></math> is superior to SCC with the exception of
the digits dataset, where <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5 achieves 0.4% better ARI and 1.2% better NMI.
Based on our results, we classify <math id="S6.SS1.p6.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="r=\{5,25,100\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mn>5</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>25</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>100</mn></mrow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">r=\{5,25,100\}</annotation></semantics></math> as low, medium, and high-quality
settings of SCC, respectively.
Lastly, compared to DBSCAN, for these datasets, HAC-based algorithms typically yield much higher quality. However, on the Wine dataset, DBSCAN finds clusterings that have up to 5.6% higher ARI and 6.5% higher NMI than TeraHAC (and also exact HAC).</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS2" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.2. </span>Scalability</h3>

<div id="S6.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Thus far, we have seen that the quality of <math id="S6.SS2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S6.SS2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1,t=0.01" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.01</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1,t=0.01</annotation></semantics></math> is
close to that of an exact HAC baseline.
Next we evaluate the scalability of our algorithm under these settings.</p>
</div>
<figure id="S6.F15" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">

</span></figcaption>

</span></figcaption>
</figure></div><div class="ltx_flex_break"></div><div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_1">

<figcaption class="ltx_caption"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 15. </span><span id="S6.F15.fig3.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Number of good and directed edges in each round
on the CW graph (<math id="S6.F15.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math>=978M, <math id="S6.F15.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics></math>=74.7B).
The plots for other graphs show similar behavior when comparing <math id="S6.F15.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S6.F15.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.F15.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>.

</span></figcaption>
</figure></div></div>
</figure>
<div id="S6.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S6.SS2.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scalability on Large Graphs.</span>
We study the scalability of <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> compared
with <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math> on the rMAT graph family described at the start
of this section.
The average-degree of these graphs is close to 100,
and is thus similar to our other real-world graphs (see Table <a href="#S5.T1" title="Table 1 ‣ 5. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       Implementation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).
Fig. <a href="#S6.F11" title="Figure 11 ‣ 6.1. Quality ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">11</span></a> shows the result of our experiments. We observe that the
running time for <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> lies between that of <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5 and <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-25. For the largest
rMAT graph that we study, which contains 268 million vertices and 25.8 billion edges,
<math id="S6.SS2.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is 11.4x faster than <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-100, 2.23x faster than <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-25, and only
1.45x slower than <math id="S6.SS2.p2.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Fig. <a href="#S6.F11" title="Figure 11 ‣ 6.1. Quality ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">11</span></a> shows the scalability of <math id="S6.SS2.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>
compared with <math id="S6.SS2.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math> on five large real-world graphs, including the largest
publicly available graph, the WebDataCommons Hyperlink graph (HL). Similar
to our results on the rMAT graph family, we find that <math id="S6.SS2.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> performs
between <math id="S6.SS2.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5 (0.67x as fast on average) and <math id="S6.SS2.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-25 (2.04x as fast on average). The algorithm is significantly
faster than <math id="S6.SS2.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-100 (8.3x faster on average), which is unable to finish within four days on HL.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS2.p4.1" class="ltx_p">Taken together with our results in Section <a href="#S6.SS1" title="6.1. Quality ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.1</span></a>,
<math id="S6.SS2.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is nearly as fast as the low-quality variant of <math id="S6.SS2.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>, while
achieving much higher quality than the high-quality variant of <math id="S6.SS2.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S6.SS2.p5.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Good Edges and Round-Complexity.</span>
As our results in Fig. <a href="#S1.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 1.2. Further Related Work ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> in Section <a href="#S1" title="1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>
show, <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> uses several orders of magnitude fewer rounds than <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{RAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{RAC}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>.
To better understand the reason behind the low round-complexity, we study the <span id="S6.SS2.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">number of good edges</span>
(i.e., <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good edges) available to the algorithm in each round.
This measure directly captures edges that can potentially participate in a merge.
Fig. <a href="#S6.F15" title="Figure 15 ‣ 6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">15</span></a> shows a representative result for the CW graph.
The plots for other graphs are similar.
The number of rounds used by <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> is significantly lower
than <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>.
Initially, the number of good edges with <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math> is one order of
magnitude larger than with <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math>.
Although <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math> uses more rounds than with <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>, as shown
in Fig. <a href="#S1.F2" title="Figure 2 ‣ 1.2. Further Related Work ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0</annotation></semantics></math> still uses
many fewer rounds than <math id="S6.SS2.p5.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{RAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖱𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{RAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, although both algorithms solve HAC exactly.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S6.SS2.p6.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S6.SS2.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Shared-Memory Performance.</span>
<math id="S6.SS2.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> has the ability to significantly reduce the number of rounds required for approximately solving HAC at a given weight threshold.
We have found that our preliminary experiments with a shared-memory implementation of the algorithm show that its low round-complexity also translates into strong shared-memory performance.
We study the scalability of <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> compared with state-of-the-art single-machine
HAC implementations, namely <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">dhulipala2021hierarchical</a>)</cite> and <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">parhac</a>)</cite> on a 72-core
Dell PowerEdge R930 (with two-way hyper-threading) with <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="4\times 2.4\mbox{GHz}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mn>2.4</mn></mrow><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mtext>GHz</mtext></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">4\times 2.4\mbox{GHz}</annotation></semantics></math>
processors and 1<span id="S6.SS2.p6.1.2" class="ltx_text">TB</span> of main memory.
We show the results of our shared-memory experiment in Fig. <a href="#S6.F12" title="Figure 12 ‣ 6.1. Quality ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">12</span></a>. <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is always
as fast or faster than all baselines, ranging from 1.45–8x speedup over <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> and 48.5–185x speedup over <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>. We also obtain between 1.04–5.28x speedup over
a version of <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{ParHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖯𝖺𝗋𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{ParHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using the same vertex pruning optimization as <math id="S6.SS2.p6.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> before processing each bucket.
<math id="S6.SS2.p6.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> seems to have significant potential in the shared-memory setting and we plan to further investigate this direction in future work.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="S6.SS3" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.3. </span>Large Scale Clustering of Web Queries</h3>

<figure id="S6.T3" class="ltx_table">
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering" style="font-size:80%;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3. </span><span id="S6.T3.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Median running times on the web-query dataset.</span></figcaption>
<table id="S6.T3.5" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_guessed_headers ltx_align_middle">
