<td id="S6.T3.5.2.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_t"><span id="S6.T3.5.2.3.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">690</span></td>
<td id="S6.T3.5.2.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_t"><span id="S6.T3.5.2.4.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">195</span></td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>
</figure>
<div id="S6.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Lastly, we study the quality of <math id="S6.SS3.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> on a large-scale dataset of web search queries.
We use a graph whose vertices represent queries, and edges connect queries of similar meaning.
The weight of each edge is computed using a machine-learned model based on BERT <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib22" title="" class="ltx_ref">devlin2018bert</a>)</cite>.
The graph has about 31 billion vertices and <math id="S6.SS3.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="8" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>8</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">8</annotation></semantics></math> trillion edges, and resembles the graph used in the evaluation of the SCC algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">monath2021scalable</a>)</cite>.
We use a maximum of <math id="S6.SS3.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="48\,000" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>48 000</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">48\,000</annotation></semantics></math> cores across <math id="S6.SS3.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="6\,000" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>6 000</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">6\,000</annotation></semantics></math> machines.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p2.1" class="ltx_p">For evaluation, we use a sample of <math id="S6.SS3.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="53\,659" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>53 659</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">53\,659</annotation></semantics></math> pairs of queries.
Each pair is assigned a human-generated binary label specifying whether the two queries likely carry the same intent and thus should belong to the same cluster.
<math id="S6.SS3.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="7104" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>7104</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">7104</annotation></semantics></math> (about <math id="S6.SS3.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="13\%" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>13</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">13\%</annotation></semantics></math>) of the pairs have positive labels, and the remaining are negative.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p3.1" class="ltx_p">In Fig. <a href="#S6.F15" title="Figure 15 ‣ 6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">15</span></a> we show precision and recall with respect to these labels for <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, SCC and DBSCAN.
We obtained our results by running <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> with <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t=0.05" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.05</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t=0.05</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon=0.1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0.1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon=0.1</annotation></semantics></math>, and SCC with 5 or 50 rounds of compression decreasing the weight threshold down to <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0.05" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0.05</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0.05</annotation></semantics></math>.
We evaluate different points on the precision-recall curve by considering different levels of clustering for SCC. For <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, different points are obtained by flattening the hierarchical clustering using different flattening thresholds.
The flattening algorithm uses batching to compute all flattenings simultaneously. Our implementation requires <math id="S6.SS3.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="140" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>140</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">140</annotation></semantics></math> minutes to compute all clusterings we present in Fig. <a href="#S6.F15" title="Figure 15 ‣ 6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">15</span></a>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p4.1" class="ltx_p">The implementation of DBSCAN that we use is a natural adaptation of the DBSCAN algorithm (which in its vanilla version takes a set of points as an input) to a setting where the input is a similarity graph.
The algorithm takes two parameters: <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="minPts" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>P</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>t</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">minPts</annotation></semantics></math>.
First, the algorithm considers each vertex a <em id="S6.SS3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">core</em> vertex if it contains at least <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="minPts" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>P</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>t</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">minPts</annotation></semantics></math> incident edges of weight <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\geq\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≥</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\geq\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math>.
Then, we find connected components of the subgraph of the input graph consisting of core points and all edges of weight <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\geq\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≥</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\geq\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> between them (using the algorithm described in <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">(<a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">lkacki2018connected</a>)</cite>).
These components form core clusters.
Next, each non-core vertex which does not have a core vertex of similarity at least <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> forms a singleton cluster.
Finally, for each remaining non-core vertex <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>, we assign <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> to the cluster of its most similar core neighbor.
In Fig. <a href="#S6.F15" title="Figure 15 ‣ 6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">15</span></a> we show results for <math id="S6.SS3.p4.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(\epsilon,minPts)\in\{(0.97,128),(0.97,256),(0.98,64),\allowbreak(0.98,96),(0.98,128),\allowbreak(0.99,32),(0.99,64),(0.99,128)\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>P</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>t</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.97,128</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.97,256</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.98</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>64</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.98</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>96</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.98,128</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.99</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>32</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.99</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>64</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>0.99,128</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(\epsilon,minPts)\in\{(0.97,128),(0.97,256),(0.98,64),\allowbreak(0.98,96),(0.98,128),\allowbreak(0.99,32),(0.99,64),(0.99,128)\}</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p5.1" class="ltx_p">The median running times for the algorithms are given in Table <a href="#S6.T3" title="Table 3 ‣ 6.3. Large Scale Clustering of Web Queries ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>.
We find that <math id="S6.SS3.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> achieves the highest recall at every value of precision in the range that we consider.
DBSCAN, while significantly faster than both <math id="S6.SS3.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> and SCC, consistently obtains over 2x smaller recall than <math id="S6.SS3.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S6.SS3.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S6.SS3.p6.1" class="ltx_p"><math id="S6.SS3.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> improves in quality over both <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5 and <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-50, in particular delivering about 20% better recall than <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5.
At the same time, it runs about 2x faster than <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-50 and about 2x slower than <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-5.
This difference in performance seems to be explained by reduction in the number of edges and nodes present in the graph for <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, as compared with SCC.
Fig. <a href="#S6.F15" title="Figure 15 ‣ 6.2. Scalability ‣ 6. Empirical Evaluation ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">15</span></a> shows the reduction in the number of edges over the rounds of both algorithms.
For example, there are 8.6 trillion edges in this graph, and after 10 rounds, <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-50 still has 561 billion edges remaining, whereas <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> only has 41 billion edges remaining, which is 13.4x lower than <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-50.
We observe a similar reduction in the number of nodes (clusters) that remain.
Both runs start with 31 billion nodes; after 10 rounds, 5.4 billion nodes remain for <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-50, whereas only 799 million nodes remain for <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>, which is 6.7x lower than <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SCC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖢𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SCC}</annotation></semantics></math>-50.
Our results show that even on extremely large real-world graphs, <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> using conservative values of <math id="S6.SS3.p6.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> and weight threshold achieves excellent scalability relative to state-of-the-art distributed algorithms, and in fact out-performs these baselines.
Crucially, these performance advantages are obtained while increasing accuracy at every point along the precision-recall tradeoff curve.</p>
</div>
</section>
</section>
<section id="S7" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7. </span>Conclusion</h2>

<div id="S7.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S7.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this paper we introduced the <math id="S7.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> algorithm and demonstrated its high quality and scalability on graphs of up to 8 trillion edges.
Our results indicate that <math id="S7.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> may be the algorithm of choice for clustering large-scale graph datasets.</p>
</div>
<div id="S7.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S7.p2.1" class="ltx_p">As a future work, it would be interesting to see whether we can theoretically bound the number of rounds required by the <math id="S7.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> algorithm (possibly for a carefully chosen graph partitioning method).
Another open question is whether <math id="S7.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{TeraHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖳𝖾𝗋𝖺𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{TeraHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> could be extended from computing the bottom (high-similarity) part of the dendrogram to computing the entire dendrogram.
Finally, we believe the notion of <math id="S7.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good merges may be useful for designing efficient HAC algorithms in other models, for example in the dynamic setting.</p>
</div>
</section>

<section id="A1" class="ltx_appendix">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_appendix"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Missing Proofs</h2>

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">See <a href="#S3.Thmtheorem2" title="Lemma 0. ‣ 3. Approximate Nearest-Neighbor Chain Algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a></p>
</div>
<div id="A1.2" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="A1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text">We prove the lemma by contradiction.
Pick the smallest <math id="A1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="j\geq l" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>≥</mo><mi>l</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">j\geq l</annotation></semantics></math> such that <math id="A1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}^{j}(v)&lt;w_{\max}^{j+1}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mi>j</mi></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>&lt;</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}^{j}(v)&lt;w_{\max}^{j+1}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
Then, <math id="A1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{j+1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{j+1}</annotation></semantics></math> is obtained from <math id="A1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{j}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{j}</annotation></semantics></math> by merging some vertices <math id="A1.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>, where <math id="A1.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(v,x\cup y)=w_{\max}^{j+1}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∪</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(v,x\cup y)=w_{\max}^{j+1}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
By <a href="#S2.Thmtheorem1" title="Definition 0 ((
            , 
          )). ‣ 2. Preliminaries ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> we have <math id="A1.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}^{j+1}(v)=w(v,x\cup y)\leq\max(w(vx),w(vy))\leq w_{\max}^{j}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mrow><mi>j</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∪</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>v</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>x</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>v</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mi>j</mi></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}^{j+1}(v)=w(v,x\cup y)\leq\max(w(vx),w(vy))\leq w_{\max}^{j}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>, a contradiction.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>

<div id="A1.3" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="A1.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text">(<math id="A1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\implies" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mo stretchy="false">⟹</mo><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\implies</annotation></semantics></math>) By the definition of <math id="A1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> we have <math id="A1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)\leq\max(w_{\max}(u),w_{\max}(v))" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)\leq\max(w_{\max}(u),w_{\max}(v))</annotation></semantics></math>.
Moreover, <math id="A1.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)\geq\max(w_{\max}(u),\allowbreak w_{\max}(v))" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)\geq\max(w_{\max}(u),\allowbreak w_{\max}(v))</annotation></semantics></math> since, by the definition of <math id="A1.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math>-good merge, we have <math id="A1.p4.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\max(w_{\max}(u),w_{\max}(v))=\min(\mathrm{M}(u),\mathrm{M}(v),w(uv))" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>min</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\max(w_{\max}(u),w_{\max}(v))=\min(\mathrm{M}(u),\mathrm{M}(v),w(uv))</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
<div id="A1.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p5.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text">(<math id="A1.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\impliedby" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mo stretchy="false">⟸</mo><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\impliedby</annotation></semantics></math>) Since <math id="A1.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)\leq w_{\max}(u)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)\leq w_{\max}(u)</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)\leq w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)\leq w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="A1.p5.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)=\max(w_{\max}(u),w_{\max}(v))" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>max</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)=\max(w_{\max}(u),w_{\max}(v))</annotation></semantics></math> implies <math id="A1.p5.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(uv)=w_{\max}(u)=w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(uv)=w_{\max}(u)=w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
By Lemma <a href="#S3.Thmtheorem3" title="Lemma 0. ‣ 3. Approximate Nearest-Neighbor Chain Algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, <math id="A1.p5.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="M(v)\geq w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">M(v)\geq w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p5.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="M(v)\geq w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≥</mo><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">M(v)\geq w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
This implies that the merge is <math id="A1.p5.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math>-good.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="A1.p6" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p6.1" class="ltx_p">See <a href="#Thmclaim1" title="Claim 1. ‣ Proof. ‣ 3. Approximate Nearest-Neighbor Chain Algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a></p>

<div id="A1.4" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="A1.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p7.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text">Consider the sequence of merges that were made to obtain the dendrogram <math id="A1.p7.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="D" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>D</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">D</annotation></semantics></math> and let <math id="A1.p7.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{n}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>′</mo></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mi>n</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,G^{\prime}_{n}</annotation></semantics></math> be the corresponding graphs.
Assume that the merge of <math id="A1.p7.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p7.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> was done in <math id="A1.p7.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> (and resulted in obtaining graph <math id="A1.p7.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{i+1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>′</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{i+1}</annotation></semantics></math>).
Let <math id="A1.p7.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}^{\prime}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}^{\prime}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> be the value of <math id="A1.p7.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> in graph <math id="A1.p7.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math>.
Graph <math id="A1.p7.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> does not necessarily have a vertex <math id="A1.p7.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>, but since we assumed that the merge of <math id="A1.p7.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p7.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> happens before the merge of <math id="A1.p7.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>y</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p7.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="A1.p7.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> contains a set of vertices <math id="A1.p7.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{t}\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{t}\}</annotation></semantics></math>, such that <math id="A1.p7.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mo>⋃</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>t</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}y_{i}=y</annotation></semantics></math>.
By <a href="#S2.Thmtheorem1" title="Definition 0 ((
            , 
          )). ‣ 2. Preliminaries ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> we have that <math id="A1.p7.m19" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(xy)=w(x,\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}y_{i})\leq\max_{i=1}^{t}w(x,y_{i})\leq w_{\max}^{\prime}(x)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo rspace="0em">,</mo><mrow><msubsup><mo>⋃</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>t</mi></msubsup><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>max</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>t</mi></msubsup><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(xy)=w(x,\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}y_{i})\leq\max_{i=1}^{t}w(x,y_{i})\leq w_{\max}^{\prime}(x)</annotation></semantics></math>, which implies:</span></p>
<table id="A1.Ex7" class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_table">

<tbody><tr class="ltx_equation ltx_eqn_row ltx_align_baseline">
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padleft"></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_align_center"><math id="A1.Ex7.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\frac{w(xy)}{\min(\mathrm{M}(x),\mathrm{M}(x^{\prime}),w(xx^{\prime}))}\leq\frac{\max(w_{\max}^{\prime}(x),w_{\max}^{\prime}(x^{\prime}))}{\min(\mathrm{M}(x),\mathrm{M}(x^{\prime}),w(xx^{\prime}))}\leq rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><msubsup><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo 1+\epsilon.</annotation></semantics></math></td>
<td class="ltx_eqn_cell ltx_eqn_center_padright"></td></tr></tbody>
</table>
<p id="A1.p7.2" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p7.2.1" class="ltx_text">Here, the second inequality follows from the fact that the merge of <math id="A1.p7.m20" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A1.p7.m21" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x^{\prime}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>x</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x^{\prime}</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="A1.p7.m22" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{\prime}_{i}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mi>i</mi><mo>′</mo></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{\prime}_{i}</annotation></semantics></math> is good.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="A1.p8" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p8.1" class="ltx_p">See <a href="#S4.Thmtheorem2" title="Lemma 0. ‣ 4. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a></p>
</div>
<div id="A1.5" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="A1.p9" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p9.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p9.1.1" class="ltx_text">We observe that whether a merge is good only depends on the vertices involved in a merge, their <math id="A1.p9.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathrm{M}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>M</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathrm{M}</annotation></semantics></math> values and their incident edges.
Hence, the lemma easily follows when <math id="A1.p9.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k=1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k=1</annotation></semantics></math>.
Let us now consider the case of <math id="A1.p9.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k=2" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k=2</annotation></semantics></math>, the argument can be easily continued inductively.
Let <math id="A1.p9.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1}</annotation></semantics></math> be the set of vertices obtained from <math id="A1.p9.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics></math> by performing merge <math id="A1.p9.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{1}</annotation></semantics></math>, and let <math id="A1.p9.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>G</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{1}</annotation></semantics></math> be the graph obtained from <math id="A1.p9.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> by performing merge <math id="A1.p9.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{1}</annotation></semantics></math>.
It is easy to see that by performing merge <math id="A1.p9.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>m</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m_{1}</annotation></semantics></math> on <math id="A1.p9.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G^{C}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>G</mi><mi>C</mi></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G^{C}</annotation></semantics></math> we obtain the graph <math id="A1.p9.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G_{1}^{C_{1}}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msubsup><mi>G</mi><mn>1</mn><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msubsup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G_{1}^{C_{1}}</annotation></semantics></math>.
That is, the graph that <math id="A1.p9.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> sees after applying the first merge, is the same graph that we would obtain if we applied the merge on the global graph, and then computed the input to <math id="A1.p9.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>.
The lemma follows.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="A1.p10" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p10.1" class="ltx_p">See <a href="#S4.Thmtheorem6" title="Lemma 0. ‣ 4.1. Flattening the Dendrogram ‣ 4. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a></p>
</div>
<div id="A1.6" class="ltx_proof">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_font_italic ltx_title_proof">Proof.</h6>
<div id="A1.p11" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A1.p11.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A1.p11.1.1" class="ltx_text">By <a href="#S4.Thmtheorem5" title="Lemma 0. ‣ 4.1. Flattening the Dendrogram ‣ 4. 
        
          TeraHAC
        
       algorithm ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Lemma</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>, flattening the dendrogram with threshold <math id="A1.p11.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>t</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t</annotation></semantics></math> only uses nodes of the dendrogram whose linkage similarity is at least <math id="A1.p11.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t/(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t/(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>.
Pruning removes vertices whose maximum incident edge has weight strictly less than <math id="A1.p11.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t^{\prime}/(1+\epsilon)\leq t/(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msup><mi>t</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t^{\prime}/(1+\epsilon)\leq t/(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>.
By <a href="#S2.Thmtheorem1" title="Definition 0 ((
            , 
          )). ‣ 2. Preliminaries ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, each merge that such vertex participates in has merge of similarity below <math id="A1.p11.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t/(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t/(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>.
Moreover, the removal of such vertices does not affect whether a merge of similarity at least <math id="A1.p11.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="t/(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">t/(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math> is good, or does not affect the edge weights participating in such merges.
The lemma follows.
∎</span></p>
</div>
</div>
</section>
<section id="A2" class="ltx_appendix">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_appendix"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span><math id="A2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math></h2>

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this section, we give a near-linear time algorithm for
computing a set of <math id="A2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good merges
by dynamically maintaining the goodness
values of the edges, which we call <math id="A2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>.
The algorithm has the following specification: it performs only
<math id="A2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good merges, and ensures that upon completion,
there are no <math id="A2.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon^{\prime})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msup><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon^{\prime})</annotation></semantics></math>-good merges for <math id="A2.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon^{\prime}=O(\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>ϵ</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon^{\prime}=O(\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math> (see Lemma <a href="#A2.Thmtheorem4" title="Lemma 0. ‣ Correctness ‣ Appendix B 
        
          SubgraphHAC ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>).
Recall that <math id="A2.p1.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is run on the
partitions (i.e., subgraphs) of the input graph, where
vertices assigned to this partition are called
<em id="A2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">active</em> and the neighbors of these vertices that
are not in this partition are <em id="A2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">inactive</em>. Furthermore,
the vertices carry their min-merge values from prior
rounds, <math id="A2.p1.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathrm{M}{}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathrm{M}{}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
In what follows, we use vertex and cluster interchangeably.
The input to <math id="A2.p1.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is therefore a graph where vertices are
marked active and inactive, where each vertex <math id="A2.p1.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> has a min-merge value
<math id="A2.p1.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathrm{M}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>M</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathrm{M}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>, as well as a corresponding cluster size.
The goal of the algorithm is to merge a subset of the good edges, and run in near-linear time.</p>
</div>
<div id="A2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="A2.p2.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Challenges.</span>
Designing a near-linear time algorithm for this problem is surprisingly non-trivial.
One challenge is that the goodness of a vertex <math id="A2.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>’s incident edges
can change significantly even without <math id="A2.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> participating in merges
itself. For example, this could occur is if a neighbor of <math id="A2.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>
merges with another vertex, resulting in the neighboring vertex
increasing in size, causing <math id="A2.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{uv}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>w</mi><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>v</mi></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{uv}</annotation></semantics></math> to decrease. A simple example, e.g.,
a sequence of <math id="A2.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n-1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n-1</annotation></semantics></math> merges of the degree-1 vertices of a star
into the center of the star illustrates that exact maintenance of
goodness values requires <math id="A2.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{2})</annotation></semantics></math> work.
Another challenge is that the goodness of all edges incident to a vertex <math id="A2.p2.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> depends on the <math id="A2.p2.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}(u)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}(u)</annotation></semantics></math> values for all neighbors of <math id="A2.p2.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>, which in turn depends on the cluster sizes of their neighbors.
Hence, the goodness of an edge incident to <math id="A2.p2.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> may change when a vertex / cluster <em id="A2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">two hops away</em> from <math id="A2.p2.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> chages its size.</p>
</div>
<div id="A2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">These examples serve to illustrate that attempting to <span id="A2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">exactly</span>
maintain the goodness values is probably hopeless.
One could ask why bother with goodness—perhaps the algorithm
can simply keep a heap induced only on the active edges and their weights?
The prior near-linear time <math id="A2.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximate HAC
algorithm <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_citep">[<a href="#bib.bib26" title="" class="ltx_ref">26</a>]</cite> used such an approach (this was
called the heap-based approach in the paper).
However, the heap-based approach alone as used in <math id="A2.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> is insufficient for
our needs, since <math id="A2.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> cannot merge any edge that has weight smaller
than a factor of <math id="A2.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math> from the current maximum weight in the graph.
Still, our algorithm borrows and builds on some ideas from the heap-based
approach. For example, it indexes a heap over the vertices, using the lowest
(i.e., best) goodness as the priority for each vertex. It also maintains
weights using partial weights, where an edge to a neighbor <math id="A2.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> of a vertex <math id="A2.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>
is stored as a <span id="A2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">partial average-linkage weight</span>, <math id="A2.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(v,x)\cdot|v|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>x</mi><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(v,x)\cdot|v|</annotation></semantics></math>, i.e. the
average-linkage weight between vertices <math id="A2.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(v,x)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>x</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(v,x)</annotation></semantics></math> but multiplied by <math id="A2.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>, the source endpoint’s cluster size.
However, many new ideas are required to efficiently
maintain the goodness values and argue that the algorithm ensures a <math id="A2.p3.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-approximation.</p>
</div>
<div id="A2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="A2.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Our Approach.</span>
We design an algorithm based on a <span id="A2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">lazy heap-based approach</span>, which
we give some high-level pseudocode for in Algorithm <a href="#alg4" title="Algorithm 4 ‣ Appendix B 
        
          SubgraphHAC ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>.
The algorithm maintains a priority queue (min heap) over the active vertices,
with a vertex’s priority corresponding to the goodness of its smallest goodness neighbor.
The algorithm cannot maintain these goodness values exactly, but instead uses
approximate goodness values, <math id="A2.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{g}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mover accent="true"><mi>g</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{g}</annotation></semantics></math>.
There are two sources of approximation that play a role.
First, similar to the <math id="A2.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SeqHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝖾𝗊𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SeqHAC}</annotation></semantics></math> algorithm, the algorithm maintains <math id="A2.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\alpha)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>α</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\alpha)</annotation></semantics></math>
approximations of the edge weights, <math id="A2.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math>,
by “broadcasting” a vertex’s cluster size to its
neighbors and updating the weights based on the next cluster size.
Here, <math id="A2.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha\geq 0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha\geq 0</annotation></semantics></math> is a parameter of <math id="A2.p4.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>𝖲𝗎𝖻𝗀𝗋𝖺𝗉𝗁𝖧𝖠𝖢</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{SubgraphHAC}</annotation></semantics></math>.
This broadcast
operation is done only when a vertex’s cluster size increases by a <math id="A2.p4.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\alpha)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>α</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\alpha)</annotation></semantics></math> factor.
Second, it maintains an approximate view of the <math id="A2.p4.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> values for each vertex <math id="A2.p4.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>, <math id="A2.p4.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
We assign every active edge (an edge between two active endpoints)
to the endpoint with larger <math id="A2.p4.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> value; call this the <span id="A2.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">assigned endpoint</span> for an edge.
Each time the <math id="A2.p4.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> value of a vertex changes by a <math id="A2.p4.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\alpha)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>α</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\alpha)</annotation></semantics></math> factor,
the algorithm goes over all assigned edges and reassigns them to whichever endpoint has higher <math id="A2.p4.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> value.
This approach yields good guarantees on (1) the approximation
quality of each edge we merge and (2) termination conditions for the algorithm (i.e., when
it terminates, no edges with goodness below a given threshold remain).
The algorithm itself (Algorithm <a href="#alg4" title="Algorithm 4 ‣ Appendix B 
        
          SubgraphHAC ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>) is relatively
simple, with most of the work being done by the graph
representation, described next.</p>
</div>

<section id="A2.SSx1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection">Graph Representation</h3>

<div id="A2.SSx1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A2.SSx1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">The graph representation takes as input parameters <math id="A2.SSx1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon\geq 0,\alpha\geq 0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon\geq 0,\alpha\geq 0</annotation></semantics></math>;
<math id="A2.SSx1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>ϵ</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> controls the accuracy and ensures that every merge
is <math id="A2.SSx1.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\epsilon)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\epsilon)</annotation></semantics></math>-good; <math id="A2.SSx1.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math> controls the frequency with which
the algorithm performs broadcasts, and affects the range of goodness
for which edges are guaranteed to be merged.</p>
</div>
<div id="A2.SSx1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="A2.SSx1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">The graph representation
(1) support queries for the approximate goodness value <math id="A2.SSx1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{g}(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>g</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{g}(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> of each edge,
(2) support queries for the <math id="A2.SSx1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}(v)</annotation></semantics></math> value for each vertex,
(3) support efficiently merging pairs of active vertex, and
(4) support querying for (approximately) the lowest goodness edge incident to a vertex <math id="A2.SSx1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math>.
It supports (1–4) above
with an overall running time of all operations over any sequence
of merges in <math id="A2.SSx1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(m+n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(m+n)</annotation></semantics></math> time.
We can support (4) only for edges with true goodness value in a given
range <math id="A2.SSx1.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="[1,T]" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>T</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">[1,T]</annotation></semantics></math>, and elaborate more on this in Lemma <a href="#A2.Thmtheorem4" title="Lemma 0. ‣ Correctness ‣ Appendix B 
        
          SubgraphHAC ‣ TeraHAC: Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering of Trillion-Edge Graphs" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>.</p>
</div>
<div id="A2.SSx1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="A2.SSx1.p3.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A2.SSx1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Data Structures and Initialization.</span>
Each active edge <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> (an edge where both <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> are active)
is initially <span id="A2.SSx1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">assigned</span> to whichever of its endpoints has larger <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> value.
Let <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="A(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">A(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> be the endpoint that the edge <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> is assigned to.
Initially, the <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> values are exact, and can be computed by
scanning all the edges.
For each active <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v\in V" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>v</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v\in V</annotation></semantics></math>, we store all edges assigned to the vertex
in a priority queue <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="A(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">A(v)</annotation></semantics></math>, which is keyed by the approximate goodness
of the edge (from smallest to largest).
Initially, the goodness value for an assigned edge <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> is <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{g}(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>g</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{g}(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math>.
We maintain for each active vertex <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>v</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v</annotation></semantics></math> its full neighborhood,
including both active and inactive neighbors.
<math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="N(v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>N</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">N(v)</annotation></semantics></math> stores the weights of <span id="A2.SSx1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">all</span> of its incident edges
using the one-sided partial weight representation: for each <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> edge incident
to <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m15" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math>, we store <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m16" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(u,v)\cdot|u|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(u,v)\cdot|u|</annotation></semantics></math>, i.e., the true average-linkage weight, <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m17" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(u,v)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>v</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(u,v)</annotation></semantics></math> multiplied by the
cluster size of <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m18" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math>.
The neighborhood is stored as a sparse set, e.g., using a hashtable.
Each active vertex <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m19" class="ltx_Math" alttext="u" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>u</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">u</annotation></semantics></math> stores two quantities:
(a) the initial cluster size of the vertex and
(b) the approximate <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m20" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> value of the vertex.
The stored values are updated whenever the cluster size of the vertex
increases sufficiently, or whenever the <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m21" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w_{\max}{}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>w</mi><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w_{\max}{}</annotation></semantics></math> value
changes sufficiently, as we discuss shortly.
Lastly, we maintain a list of edges <math id="A2.SSx1.p3.m22" class="ltx_Math" alttext="R" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>R</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">R</annotation></semantics></math> to be <span id="A2.SSx1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">reassigned</span>
which is cleared at the end of each merge.</p>
</div>
<div id="A2.SSx1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="A2.SSx1.p4.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="A2.SSx1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Invariants.</span>
The invariants are motivated by the following two broadcast
operations, which update information along all neighboring
edges whenever a vertex’s cluster size or <math id="A2.SSx1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{w}_{\max}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>w</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mi>max</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{w}_{\max}</annotation></semantics></math> value
change by a <math id="A2.SSx1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\alpha)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>α</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\alpha)</annotation></semantics></math> factor.
The first type of broadcast updates a node’s cluster size in
all of its neighbor’s data structures after its cluster size increases
by a <math id="A2.SSx1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(1+\alpha)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mi>α</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(1+\alpha)</annotation></semantics></math>-factor.
